_source line 1206 · 2026-05-26T10:16:24.399Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   This is the final phase of an EMNLP 2026 paper submission for "Temporal Score Incomparability in LLM Benchmarks." The conversation covers multiple sequential pre-submission tasks:
   
   a) **Complete data audit report** — Deliver the 4-item audit report (total numbers audited, inconsistencies, how handled, numbers without clear source). All 5 audit groups were already completed in the prior session.
   
   b) **S3 backup** — Package all non-public project data into a tarball and upload to S3 (`s3://agent-ml-manager-backups-emnlp/manager-backup/backends/irt_equated_bench_regen/project-archive/`). Exclude public datasets (metabench_data), caches, venvs.
   
   c) **Final review for score only** — Submit paper to internal review, but paper is FROZEN — only extract the score, do NOT modify paper or create revision plans regardless of review findings.
   
   d) **AI Disclosure** — Add exact verbatim one-line AI disclosure section to end of appendix. Do not embellish.
   
   e) **Ethics/Limitations position check** — Verify Limitations is after Conclusion before References; check if Ethical Considerations needed.
   
   f) **OpenReview submission form** — Fill out complete ARR/EMNLP 2026 submission form including metadata, policy fields, and responsible research checklist.
   
   g) **Anonymization scan** — Check all .tex, .bib, comments, PDF metadata for dates or internal identifiers that could de-anonymize.
   
   h) **Forward reference check** — Scan for "used before defined" issues with abbreviations, symbols, terms.
   
   i) **Citation verification** — Dispatch 2-3 independent workers to cross-verify every citation in the .bib file against actual publications.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis
   - ETS (Educational Testing Service) A/B/C severity classification
   - DEFF (Design Effect) correction for non-independence
   - METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models × 12,508 MMLU items)
   - Cross-subject matching with ΔFPR=0 guarantee
   - Semi-synthetic injection validation framework
   - EMNLP 2026 ARR submission: 8-page main text limit, Limitations/Ethics don't count
   - ACL template with `\usepackage[review]{acl}`
   - ARR Responsible Research Checklist (sections A-E)
   - OpenReview submission form fields and policies
   - S3 backup via boto3 (PUT-only credentials)

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`paper/sections/introduction.tex`**
     - Two forward-reference fixes applied
     - Fix 1: `"with ETS severity classification"` → `"with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification"`
     - Fix 2: `"29.2% after DEFF correction"` → `"29.2% after design-effect (DEFF) correction"`
   
   - **`paper/sections/appendix.tex`** (lines 394-397)
     - Replaced detailed AI disclosure with user's verbatim one-liner
     - Old: Multi-item detailed section with `\label{app:ai-assistants}` listing Code/Writing/Literature uses
     - New (exact):
     ```latex
     \section{Use of AI Assistance}

     We used Claude for coding, code drafting, debugging, and language polishing.
     ```
   
   - **`paper/sections/method.tex`** — Read for audit/forward-reference checks, no changes
   - **`paper/sections/experiments.tex`** — Read for audit/forward-reference checks, no changes
   - **`paper/sections/discussion.tex`** — Read to verify A2 claim; §5.2 is audit protocol not risk discussion
   - **`paper/sections/conclusion.tex`** — Read for forward-reference check, no changes
   - **`paper/sections/limitations.tex`** — Verified position (after Conclusion, before References) ✓
   - **`paper/sections/related_work.tex`** — Read for forward-reference check, no changes
   - **`paper/sections/abstract.tex`** — Read for form filling, no changes
   - **`paper/main.tex`** — Structure: abstract → intro → related_work → method → experiments → discussion → conclusion → limitations → \bibliography → \appendix → appendix
   - **`paper/references.bib`** — 55 entries, read for citation verification (in progress)
   - **`/tmp/upload_to_s3.py`** — boto3 upload script (created and used)
   - **`/tmp/irt_equated_bench_regen-final-20260525T205807Z.tar.gz`** — 269MB backup tarball

4. Errors and fixes:

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we assume that every test item measures the same construct for both models. In educational testing, this property, \\emph{measurement invariance}~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, is routinely verified before scores are compared across groups; in LLM evaluation, it has never been tested at scale. We test it here, applying Mantel-Haenszel differential item functioning (DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items). The result is sobering: 31\\% of items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, compared to 0.62\\% under random-split controls, and the finding replicates across all six METABENCH benchmarks (17.9--31.3\\%).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 31.3\\% of items flagged as C (29.2\\% after design-effect (DEFF) correction), yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThis instability is not what it appears. Aggregate rankings are near-perfect ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$): even after all unstable items are removed, the leaderboard barely changes. But individual items have shifted in structured ways: some became easier for newer models independent of overall ability, others harder, and these differential effects can shift individual models by ${\\pm}561$ rank positions at the 99th percentile (\\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Instability does not threaten validity; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), which confirms structured capability changes, not noise.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThis diagnostic signal is distinct from what existing benchmark integrity methods target. Contamination detection asks whether items appear in training corpora, probing \\emph{global exposure}~\\citep{shi2024mink,dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Measurement invariance asks whether items \\emph{function equivalently} across model cohorts (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard~$= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones. Any item-level detector validated solely against web-overlap labels is blind to differential functioning.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts due to disproportionate exposure. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTo our knowledge, this is the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementary to concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 31.3\\% of MMLU items exhibit large temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks; 29.2\\% after non-independence correction), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), consistent with structured capability shifts rather than uniform exposure (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: cross-subject matching eliminates false-positive inflation under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), portable across benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens with actionable audit protocol}: unstable items carry disproportionate cross-cohort signal ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$) yet are the least ranking-disruptive under non-random removal, which confirms that DIF isolates differential functioning rather than generally informative items; we propose a Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol for benchmark maintainers (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 34,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 34
      }
    },

  },

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, pointing to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not a cohort-structure artifact (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}). Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: C\\% (29.2\\%) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}\n\\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\\ random item removal. DIF-ordered removal (orange) degrades $\\rho$ faster than random (blue band = mean $\\pm$ 1\\,SD, 20 seeds). Dashed lines: Monitor/Flag (15\\%) and Flag/Act (25\\%) thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).}\n\\label{fig:rho_degradation}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \\geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and area under the curve (AUC) of 0.93--0.94 for $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\\sim}$29\\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[29.2\\%, 37.8\\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$; \\Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\\% and weighted-mean yields 13.5\\%, bracketing C\\% at 13.5--29.2\\%; the cluster bootstrap CI excludes the weighted-mean estimate, providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound (\\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Random permutations within the 2023 cohort confirm the ETS~C threshold maintains per-item FPR below 1\\% (99th percentile $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| = 1.45$); no non-dominant family drives the signal (leave-one-family-out [LOFO] $|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp; \\Cref{tab:lofo,app:dedup_k}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\\% of 0.62\\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\\times$ below temporal C\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$; at most ${\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. C\\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \\citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\\ labels are not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & C\\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,090 / ${\\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2024 (sanity) & ${\\sim}$400 / ${\\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; $p = 0.88$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Label-independent evidence further supports mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$; \\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning}: complementary diagnostic dimensions (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$; cross-subject C\\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\\% converges to ${\\sim}$28--29\\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\\Cref{fig:matching}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \\leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$, still insufficient to account for the observed 31\\% C\\% (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% (\\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\\rho = 0.997$, mean $\\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\\leq 2.9\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average (\\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks, including ARC (AI2 Reasoning Challenge), consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks (2023 vs.\\ 2024 cohorts). $\\rho$/$\\tau$: rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip: pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio: C\\%/random baseline.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).\n",
        "numLines": 102,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 102
      }

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh,magis2010difr} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}; $K{=}3$ yields near-identical power (\\Cref{app:k3_power}). Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. At $N = 1{,}887$ (the temporal comparison; 5{,}227 total across all cohort designs), significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\\%) lose significance while retaining $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 74\\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\\mathrm{adj}} \\in [0.05, 0.10]$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ets_distribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Distribution of $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across 12,508 MMLU items (2023 vs.\\ 2024 cohorts). Colors indicate ETS severity: A (negligible, $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$), B (moderate), C (large, $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and $p < 0.05$). The heavy tails show that 31.3\\% of items exhibit large DIF, 50$\\times$ above random baselines (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).}\n\\label{fig:ets_distribution}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on Grade School Math 8K (GSM8K) items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is adequate: IRT $\\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\\Cref{sec:structural}).\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces four challenges absent in educational testing. First, overlapping training corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1{,}000 replications shuffling group labels within score strata) confirms local-dependence-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$~pp under cross-subject matching (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, bimodal ability distributions motivate quintile-based stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57-subject structure raises a multidimensionality concern~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf,roussos1996multidimensionality}: domain-level ability profiles are nearly flat across cohorts (Cohen's $d$ range $= 0.12$), cross-subject and within-subject matching agree ($\\kappa = 0.80$, 834 items), and cross-subject-unique C items do not concentrate in high-shift subjects (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check. Calendar-year boundaries are coarse; half-yearly comparison (C\\% $= 34.4\\%$; \\Cref{app:uncertainty}) and base-only/instruct-only analyses (C\\% $= 32.1\\%$/$36.5\\%$; \\Cref{app:baseinst_temporal}) confirm DIF persists under finer slicing.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ ($= \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{injected}} - \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{baseline}}$) increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$ under binary injection (a calibration upper bound; \\Cref{sec:injection}); MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nWe use contamination injection as a controlled case study to validate DIF detection power; DIF itself is mechanism-agnostic and flags items that function differently without attributing cause (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}). To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a stylized uniform injection pattern, reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power; realistic contamination (partial memorization, format-specific advantages) would produce weaker signals.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$). $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ is a constructive guarantee of the matching strategy, independent of injection realism, whereas $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ may overestimate detection power under the stylized uniform injection. $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ remains negligible for single-domain contamination ($k \\leq 5$); when contamination spans many domains, within-subject matching should be preferred (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}). We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol.\n\nFollowing DIF convention~\\citep{wainer1993differential}, we do not apply family-wise error correction: the dual ETS~C threshold ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ \\emph{and} $p < 0.05$) guards against false positives; Benjamini-Hochberg false discovery rate correction at $q = 0.05$ reclassifies only 3 of 3{,}918 C items (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Diagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3, domain-specificity, DIF decomposition) and further details appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching ($p = 0.5$). Each arrow connects the same subject under both strategies. Cross-subject points (filled) cluster at $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ while retaining $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects; within-subject points (open) inflate FPR by up to ${+}0.12$.}\n\\label{fig:matching}\n\\end{figure}\n",
        "numLines": 80,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 80
      }
    },

Links: [{"title":"[2510.02386] On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02386"},{"title":"On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2510.02386v1"},{"title":"On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models","url":"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/396223620_On_The_Fragility_of_Benchmark_Contamination_Detection_in_Reasoning_Models"},{"title":"[PDF] On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models | Semantic Scholar","url":"https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/On-The-Fragility-of-Benchmark-Contamination-in-Wang-Li/9a5bf5d3c7116cc4a104c06599fbcef2d1b5abf3"},{"title":"DynaSolidGeo: A Dynamic Benchmark for Genuine Spatial Mathematical Reasoning of VLMs in Solid Geometry","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2510.22340v1"},{"title":"On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=bhR00j6Mku"},{"title":"[2510.09259] Detecting Data Contamination from Reinforcement Learning Post-training for Large Language Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09259"}]

Based on the search results, here's information about arxiv 2510.02386:

## Paper Overview

The paper addresses how leaderboards for large reasoning models (LRMs) have turned evaluation into a competition, incentivizing developers to optimize directly on benchmark suites.

## Main Problem

A shortcut to achieving higher rankings is to incorporate evaluation benchmarks into the training data, thereby yielding inflated performance, known as benchmark contamination. Surprisingly, the studies find that evading contamination detections for LRMs is alarmingly easy.

## Key Findings

The paper reveals that existing contamination detection methods are fragile under LRM contamination scenarios: RL conceals SFT contamination evidence introduced during the transition from base models to LRMs, while contamination with CoT applied to advanced LRMs leaves little detectable evidence.

More specifically:
- While SFT contamination to the base model is initially detectable, contamination evidence can be concealed through subsequent RL training.
- Extensive contamination with CoT on advanced LRMs barely leaves evidence for existing memorization-driven detection methods.

## Implications

These findings underscore the urgent need for advanced contamination detection methods and trustworthy evaluation protocols tailored to LRMs.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Based on the search results, I found the paper you're looking for:

**DVD: A Robust Method for Detecting Variant Contamination in Large Language Model Evaluation** (arXiv:2601.04895)

The paper addresses variant contamination in LLM evaluation, where the training corpus contains semantically equivalent yet lexically or syntactically altered versions of test items. Unlike verbatim leakage, these paraphrased or structurally transformed variants evade existing detectors based on sampling consistency or perplexity, thereby inflating benchmark scores via memorization rather than genuine reasoning.

The authors introduce DVD (Detection via Variance of generation Distribution), a single-sample detector that models the local output distribution induced by temperature sampling. Their key insight is that contaminated items trigger alternation between a memory-adherence state and a perturbation-drift state, yielding abnormally high variance in the synthetic difficulty of low-probability tokens; uncontaminated items remain in drift with comparatively smooth variance.

The researchers construct the first benchmark for variant contamination across two domains—Omni-MATH and SuperGPQA—by generating and filtering semantically equivalent variants, and simulate contamination via fine-tuning models of different scales and architectures (Qwen2.5 and Llama3.1). Across datasets and models, DVD consistently outperforms perplexity-based, Min-k% probability, edit-distance, and embedding-similarity baselines, while exhibiting strong robustness to hyperparameters.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Based on the search results, here's what I found about arxiv 2407.12844:

## Overview

This paper introduces metabench, a sparse benchmark engineered to distill the essence of six prominent benchmarks—ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA, and WinoGrande—into a more concise and informative benchmark.

## Key Findings

The authors used data from more than 5000 LLMs to identify the most informative items of six benchmarks (with 28,632 items in total), and distilled a sparse benchmark, metabench, that has less than 3% of the original size of all six benchmarks combined.

The estimators can be used to reconstruct each original individual benchmark score with, on average, 1.5% root mean square error (RMSE), reconstruct the original total score with 0.8% RMSE, and have a single underlying common factor whose Spearman correlation with the total score is r = 0.93.

## Authors and Publication

The paper was authored by Alex Kipnis, Konstantinos Voudouris, Luca M. Schulze Buschoff, and Eric Schulz, and was accepted for publication at ICLR 2025.

Based on the search results, I found information about the paper you're looking for:

The paper "metabench -- A Sparse Benchmark of Reasoning and Knowledge in Large Language Models" (arxiv 2407.12844) is authored by Alex Kipnis and 3 other authors. The full author list includes Alex Kipnis, Konstantinos Voudouris, Luca M. Schulze Buschoff, and Eric Schulz from Human-Centered AI at Helmholtz Munich (and Voudouris also from the University of Cambridge).

The research uses data from over 5000 LLMs to identify the most informative items from six benchmarks (ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA and WinoGrande), distilling a sparse benchmark called metabench that has less than 3% of the original size of all six benchmarks combined.

Note: There is no "Alon Kipnis" associated with this paper—the first author is **Alex Kipnis**.

Links: [{"title":"[2510.02386] On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02386"},{"title":"On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=bhR00j6Mku"},{"title":"On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models","url":"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/396223620_On_The_Fragility_of_Benchmark_Contamination_Detection_in_Reasoning_Models"},{"title":"[PDF] On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models | Semantic Scholar","url":"https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/On-The-Fragility-of-Benchmark-Contamination-in-Wang-Li/9a5bf5d3c7116cc4a104c06599fbcef2d1b5abf3"},{"title":"On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2510.02386v1"},{"title":"Carnegie Mellon at ICLR 2026 – Machine Learning Blog | ML@CMU | Carnegie Mellon University","url":"https://blog.ml.cmu.edu/2026/04/20/carnegie-mellon-at-iclr-2026/"}]

I found the paper you're looking for! Here's the information about it:

The paper "On The Fragility of Benchmark Contamination Detection in Reasoning Models" was authored by Han Wang (University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign), Haoyu Li (University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign), Brian Ko (University of Washington), and Huan Zhang (CMU), and is accepted at ICLR 2026.

The paper addresses how leaderboards for large reasoning models (LRMs) have turned evaluation into a competition, with benchmark contamination—incorporating evaluation benchmarks into training data to yield inflated performance—being a significant concern, and the authors find that evading contamination detections for LRMs is alarmingly easy.

The paper focuses on two scenarios: when the base model evolves into an LRM via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL), contamination during SFT can be identified by detection methods, but even brief Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) training can conceal contamination signals that most detection methods rely on.

The paper is available on arXiv (paper ID 2510.02386) and OpenReview.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

I found the paper you're looking for! Here are the details:

**Paper:** "Efficient Detection of Bad Benchmark Items with Novel Scalability Coefficients"

**arXiv ID:** 2603.24999

**Authors:** Michael Hardy, Joshua Gilbert, Benjamin Domingue

**Key contribution:** The paper introduces a new family of nonparametric scalability coefficients based on interitem isotonic regression for efficiently detecting globally bad items (e.g., miskeyed, ambiguously worded, or construct-misaligned).

**Focus:** The paper focuses on global bad-item detection: efficiently prioritizing items for human review when some items are flawed in ways that degrade measurement overall.

The paper was submitted on March 26, 2026, with a revised version on March 27, 2026. You can access it directly at https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24999.

I found the paper you're looking for. Here's what I can tell you about it:

"Quantifying Memorization Across Neural Language Models" (arxiv 2202.07646) addresses how large language models memorize parts of their training data and emit memorized training data verbatim when prompted appropriately.

Memorization is undesirable because it violates privacy (exposing user data), degrades utility (repeated easy-to-memorize text is often low quality), and hurts fairness (some texts are memorized over others).

The paper describes three log-linear relationships that quantify memorization, finding that it significantly grows as they increase: (1) the capacity of a model, (2) the number of times an example has been duplicated, and (3) the number of tokens of context used to prompt the model.

The paper was published as a conference paper at ICLR 2023. The authors are Nicholas Carlini, Daphne Ippolito, Matthew Jagielski, Katherine Lee, Florian Tramer, and Chiyuan Zhang.

The paper is available at https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.07646.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Based on the search results, I found information about the paper you're looking for:

CoDeC (Contamination Detection via Context) is a practical and accurate method to detect and quantify training data contamination in large language models. CoDeC distinguishes between data memorized during training and data outside the training distribution by measuring how in-context learning affects model performance.

In-context examples typically boost confidence for unseen datasets but may reduce it when the dataset was part of training, due to disrupted memorization patterns. CoDeC achieves near-perfect dataset-level AUC of 99.9% across many models, while baseline methods fail.

The method is simple, automated, and both model- and dataset-agnostic, making it easy to integrate with benchmark evaluations.

The paper appears to be available on both arXiv (2510.27055) and OpenReview, with the OpenReview version indicating it was submitted to an ICLR workshop.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

I found the paper! The paper "Confident Rankings with Fewer Items: Adaptive LLM Evaluation with Continuous Scores" (arXiv:2601.13885) was authored by Esma Balkır, Alice Pernthaller, Marco Basaldella, José Hernández-Orallo, and Nigel Collier.

The paper presents a principled extension of IRT-based adaptive testing to continuous bounded scores (ROUGE, BLEU, LLM-as-a-Judge) by replacing the Bernoulli response distribution with a heteroskedastic normal distribution. The authors introduce an uncertainty aware ranker with adaptive stopping criteria that achieves reliable model ranking while testing as few items and as cheaply as possible.

_source line 1353 · 2026-05-26T10:23:03.903Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TEdhBerboZsEB94hgjMEEE`</summary>

```text
sections/introduction.tex:18:This diagnostic signal is distinct from what existing benchmark integrity methods target. Contamination detection asks whether items appear in training corpora, probing \emph{global exposure}~\citep{shi2024mink,dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\citep{li2024contamination}. Measurement invariance asks whether items \emph{function equivalently} across model cohorts (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard~$= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones. Any item-level detector validated solely against web-overlap labels is blind to differential functioning.
sections/related_work.tex:10:A growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training-data analysis. Membership inference approaches use token-level log-likelihood statistics \citep{shi2024mink} or statistical anomalies \citep{dekoninck2024constat} to flag items likely seen during training. A standard validation practice is to compare detector output against web-overlap labels \citep{li2024contamination}, which classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts. This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our comparison with web-overlap labels (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). \citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.

Contamination detection asks whether items appear in training corpora, probing \\emph{global exposure}~\\citep{shi2024mink,dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Measurement invariance asks whether items \\emph{function equivalently} across model cohorts (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard~$= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones. Any item-level detector validated solely against web-overlap labels is blind to differential functioning.\nsections/related_work.tex:10:A growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training-data analysis. Membership inference approaches use token-level log-likelihood statistics \\citep{shi2024mink} or statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} to flag items likely seen during training. A standard validation practice is to compare detector output against web-overlap labels \\citep{li2024contamination}, which classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts. This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our comparison with web-overlap labels (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DMzdLWQckTft8o4pzEvrta`</summary>

```text
sections/experiments.tex:4:We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
sections/introduction.tex:5:When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we assume that every test item measures the same construct for both models. In educational testing, this property, \emph{measurement invariance}~\citep{meredith1993invariance}, is routinely verified before scores are compared across groups; in LLM evaluation, it has never been tested at scale. We test it here, applying Mantel-Haenszel differential item functioning (DIF) analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items). The result is sobering: 31\% of items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, compared to 0.62\% under random-split controls, and the finding replicates across all six METABENCH benchmarks (17.9--31.3\%).
sections/method.tex:9:The primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.

I found the HellaSwag paper! Here are the details:

The paper "HellaSwag: Can a Machine Really Finish Your Sentence?" was authored by Rowan Zellers, Ari Holtzman, Yonatan Bisk, Ali Farhadi, and Yejin Choi, and was published at the Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics in 2019.

It appears in the Proceedings of the 57th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics, pages 4791–4800, held in Florence, Italy.

The paper presents HellaSwag, a new challenge dataset showing that commonsense inference still proves difficult for state-of-the-art models, though the questions are trivial for humans with >95% accuracy while models struggle at <48%. The authors achieved this through Adversarial Filtering (AF), a data collection paradigm where a series of discriminators iteratively select an adversarial set of machine-generated wrong answers.

The paper is available on arXiv (1905.07830) and has been widely cited in the NLP community.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

Han, 2025","url":"https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.3102/10769986241269820"},{"title":"A Statistical Test for Differential Item Pair Functioning | Psychometrika","url":"https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11336-014-9408-y"}]

Based on the search results, I found the article you're looking for:

**"Rethinking Item Fairness Using Single World Intervention Graphs"** by Youmi Suk and Weicong Lyu, published in 2026.

The study introduces a novel causal framework for defining and detecting unfair items using single-world intervention graphs (SWIGs), and defines causal DIF (CDIF) as the difference in item functioning between two hypothetical worlds: one where individuals were assigned to one group and another where they were assigned to a different group.

The authors note that traditional DIF analyses focus only on statistical relationships in observed data and cannot explain why such unfairness occurs, which is the gap their causal framework aims to address.

The article appears to be published in the Journal of Educational and Behavioral Statistics rather than Psychometrika. You can access it at: https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.3102/10769986251415570


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Based on the search results, I found information about the Roussos and Stout 1996 publication in the Journal of Educational Measurement, volume 33. 

Roussos and Stout published a 1996 article titled "Simulation studies of the effects of small sample size and studied item parameters on SIBTEST and Mantel-Haenszel Type 1 error performance" in the Journal of Education Measurement, 33, 215–230.

This article appears in multiple citations across the search results and is a well-established reference in the psychometric literature on differential item functioning (DIF). The Roussos-Stout (1996) multidimensionality-based DIF analysis framework emphasizes a substantively-informed selection of items for both the matching and studied subtest based on the dimensions.

The search results also reference other related work by Roussos and Stout from 1996, including a paper titled "DIF from the multidimensional perspective" published in Applied Psychological Measurement, 20, 335–371.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Based on the search results, here's what I found about MMLU-CF:

## Overview

MMLU-CF is a contamination-free and more challenging MCQ benchmark that reassesses LLMs' understanding of world knowledge by averting both unintentional and malicious data leakage.

## Authors and Publication

The paper was authored by Qihao Zhao, Yangyu Huang, Tengchao Lv, Lei Cui, Qinzheng Sun, Shaoguang Mao, Xin Zhang, Ying Xin, Qiufeng Yin, Scarlett Li, and Furu Wei. The paper was published at ACL 2025, and the arXiv preprint is numbered 2412.15194. The authors are from Microsoft Research.

## Key Features

To avoid unintentional data leakage, the benchmark sources data from a broader domain and designs three decontamination rules. The test set remains closed-source to ensure reliable results, while the validation set is publicly available to promote transparency and facilitate independent evaluation.

## Evaluation Results

Over 40 mainstream LLMs were evaluated on MMLU-CF, and compared to the original MMLU, not only LLMs' performances significantly dropped but also the performance rankings of them changed considerably.
